Appendix H

Proofs

H.1

Proof of Lemma
1

Fix any
v
. First we show that if (
7
) holds for all
r
then Assumption MONO holds. Indeed, suppose that for some
h^{\prime}>h
we had
r(h^{\prime},v)<r(h,v)
. Substituting
r=r(h^{\prime},v)
into (
7
), we would then have that
r(h,v)>r(h^{\prime},v)\implies h>\tau_{v}(r(h^{\prime},v))
and hence that
h^{\prime}>\tau_{v}(r(h^{\prime},v))
since
h^{\prime}>h
. But
h^{\prime}>\tau_{v}(r(h^{\prime},v))
violates the definition of
\tau_{v}
, since then
h^{\prime}>\sup\{h\in\mathcal{H}:r(h,v)\leq r(h^{\prime},v)\}\geq h^{\prime}
.

Left-continuity of
r
holds by considering any increasing sequence of
h
converging to
\tau_{v}(r)
, i.e. I show that
\lim_{h\uparrow\tau_{v}(r)}r(h,v)=r(\tau_{v}(r),v)
. First, note that
\lim_{h\uparrow\tau_{v}(r)}r(h,v)>r(\tau_{v}(r),v)
would violate weak monotonicity of
r
. Suppose instead that
\lim_{h\uparrow\tau_{v}(r)}r(h,v)=r^{*}
where
r^{*}<r(\tau_{v}(r),v)
. This limit exists by the increasing property of
r
. It must then be the case that
\tau_{v}(r^{*})=\tau_{v}(r)
. To see this, consider the two alternatives. For
\tau_{v}(r^{*})<\tau_{v}(r)
, there would need to exist an
h^{*}
such that
r(h^{*},v)>r^{*}
but
h^{*}<\tau_{v}(r)
. This would violate
\lim_{h\uparrow\tau_{v}(r)}r(h,v)=r^{*}
given that
r
is increasing. Suppose instead that
\tau_{v}(r^{*})>\tau_{v}(r)
. Then there would need to exist an
h^{*}
such that
r(h^{*},v)>r
but
h^{*}<\tau_{v}(r)
. But
h^{*}<\tau_{v}(r)
implies that
r(h^{*},v)\leq r
given that
r
is increasing. Now, given that
\tau_{v}(r^{*})=\tau_{v}(r)
,
r^{*}<r(\tau_{v}(r),v)
would violate (
7
) for
h=\tau_{v}(r^{*})
, because
r(h,v)>r\implies h>\tau_{v}(r)
.

Now we will show that if Assumption MONO holds then (
7
) is satisfied for all
v,r
. First, note that
\tau_{v}(r)
is weakly increasing in
r
, and thus
r(h,v)\leq r\implies\tau_{v}(r(h,v))\leq\tau_{v}(r)\implies h\leq\tau_{v}(r)
since by the definition of
\tau_{v}(r)
:
h\leq\tau_{v}(r(h,v))
for any
h
. Thus we can establish the
\implies
direction of (
7
), without even invoking Assumption MONO. In the other direction, assume that for some
r
and
h
,
h\leq\tau_{v}(r)
but
r(h,v)>r
. By the increasing property of MONO:
h\leq\tau_{v}(r)\implies r(h,v)\leq r(\tau_{v}(r),v)
. Thus
r<r(h,v)\leq r(\tau_{v}(r),v)
and thus
r(\tau_{v}(r),v)>r
, so
r(\cdot,v)
must have a left discontinuity at
\tau_{v}(r)
.

H.2

Proof of Theorem
1

Along the way to proving Theorem
1
, we will first establish the following result:

Lemma 2
.

Assume MONO holds and that REG
j
holds for some
j\in\{1,\dots,J\}
. Assume further that
\{X_{i}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp V_{i}\}\textrm{ }|\textrm{ }W_{i}
(the first part of EXOG). Then:

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{j}}

\displaystyle P(R_{i}\leq r|x,w)

\displaystyle=-\mathbbm{E}\left\{\left.{f_{H}(\tau_{V_{i}}(r)|x,V_{i},w)}\cdot\partial_{x_{j}}\left.Q_{H|XVW}(\alpha|x,V_{i},w)\right|_{\alpha=F_{H|XVW}(\tau_{V_{i}}(r)|x,V_{i},w)]}\right|W_{i}=w\right\}

Lemma
2
is of independent interest, because it shows that under MONO, a regression of the distribution of
R
on a component of
X
can be decomposed into a linear combination of quantile regressions of
H
on
X
(conditional on
V
and
W
). Beyond regularity conditions, this result only requires reporting heterogeneity
V
to be conditionally independent of variation in
X_{j}
, and no causal assumptions. To interpret this result causally, we add the second part of EXOG and establish Theorem
1
.

To establish both results, note that by the law of iterated expectation and Lemma
1
:

\displaystyle P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i}=x,W_{i}=w)

\displaystyle=\int dF_{UV|XW}(u,v|x,w)\cdot\mathbbm{1}(r(h(x,u),v)\leq r)

\displaystyle=\int dF_{UV|XW}(u,v|x,w)\cdot\mathbbm{1}(h(x,u)\leq\tau_{v}(r))

\displaystyle=\int dF_{V|XW}(v|x,w)\int dF_{U|XVW}(u|x,v,w)\cdot\mathbbm{1}(h(x,u)\leq\tau_{v}(r))

\displaystyle=\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\mathbbm{E}\left[\left.\mathbbm{1}(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r))\right|X_{i}=x,V_{i}=v,W_{i}=w\right]

\displaystyle=\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot P(H_{i}\leq\tau_{v}(r)|X_{i}=x,V_{i}=v,W_{i}=w)

where I have used
\left\{X_{i}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp V_{i}\right\}|W_{i}
in the second to last step to replace
F_{V|XW}
with
F_{V|W}
.

By differentiating the equation
Q_{H|XVW}(F_{H|XVW}(h|x,v)|x,v,w)=h
with respect to
x_{j}
, we have:

\partial_{x_{j}}P(H_{i}\leq h|X_{i}=x,V_{i}=v,W_{i}=w)=-f_{H}(h|x,v,w)\cdot\left.\partial_{x_{j}}Q_{H|XVW}(\alpha|x,v,w)\right|_{\alpha=F_{H|XVW}(h|x,v,w)}

By dominated convergence (using Assumption REG) we can move the derivative inside the expectation, and thus:

\partial_{x_{j}}P(R_{i}\leq r|x)=-\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot f_{H}(\tau_{v}(r)|x,v,w)\cdot\partial_{x_{j}}\left.Q_{H|XVW}(\alpha|x,v,w)\right|_{\alpha=F_{H|XVW}(\tau_{v}(r)|x,v,w)}

establishing Lemma
2
.

Note that EXOG implies that
\{X_{ji}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp U_{i}\}|(X_{-j,i},V_{i},W_{i})
, where
X_{-j,i}
denotes all of the components of
X_{i}
aside from the
j^{th}
. The theorem of
Hoderlein & Mammen 2007
implies that given this and REG:

\partial_{x_{j}}\left.Q_{H|XVW}(\alpha|x,v,w)\right|_{\alpha=F_{H|XVW}(\tau_{v}(r)|x,v,w)}=\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})|H_{i}=\tau_{v}(r),x,v,w\right]

Therefore:

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{j}}

\displaystyle P(R_{i}\leq r|x)=-\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot f_{H}(\tau_{v}(r)|x,v)\cdot\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})|H_{i}=\tau_{v}(r),x,v\right]

In the case where
V
is degenerate, a similar proof to the above is used in
Chernozhukov et al. 2019
to study derivatives of conditional choice probabilities in multinomial choice models (under somewhat different regularity conditions).

In the proof of Theorem 1 in
Hoderlein & Mammen 2007
, the conditional expectation function analogous to
\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})|H_{i}=h,X_{i}=x,V_{i}=v,W_{i}=w\right]
appearing in the expression for
\partial_{x_{j}}P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i}=x)
is defined to be the following integral:

\displaystyle\int dt\cdot t\cdot

\displaystyle\frac{f_{H,\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U)|XVW}(h,t|x,v,w)}{f_{H|XVW}(h|x,v,w)}=\int dt\cdot t\cdot\frac{\partial_{t}\partial_{h}P(H_{i}\leq h,\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})\leq t|x,v,w)}{\partial_{h}P(H_{i}\leq h|x,v,w)}

\displaystyle\int dt\cdot t\cdot\partial_{t}\left\{\frac{\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}P(H_{i}\in[h,h+\epsilon],\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})\leq t|x,v,w)/\epsilon}{\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}P(H_{i}\in[h,h+\epsilon]|x,v,w)/\epsilon}\right\}

\displaystyle\int dt\cdot t\cdot\partial_{t}\left\{\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{P(\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})\leq t,h(x,U_{i})\in[h,h+\epsilon]|x,v,w)}{P(h(x,U_{i})\in[h,h+\epsilon]|x,v,w)}\right\}

(50)

where
f_{H|XVW}
and
f_{H,\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U)|XVW}
exist and have a ratio that is dominated by an absolutely integrable function
M\cdot c(t)
, by Assumption
REG_{j}
. Given that
U_{i}
is a random vector in
\mathbbm{R}^{d_{U}}
with a well-defined probability distribution conditional on
X_{i}=x,V_{i}=v,W_{i}=w
, the limit

\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{P(\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})\leq t,h(x,U_{i})\in(h,h+\epsilon]|x,v,w)}{P(h(x,U_{i})\in(h,h+\epsilon]|x,v,w)}

yields a regular conditional probability distribution of
\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})
given
H_{i}=h(x,U_{i})=h
(and
X_{i}=x,V_{i}=v,W_{i}=w
). See the result of
Pfanzagl 1979
for details.

Under an interchange of the limit and the integral in (
50
), we could also write the quantity
\mathbbm{E}[\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})|H_{i}=h,X_{i}=x,V_{i}=v,W_{i}=w]
as
\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\hskip 0.0pt\mathbbm{E}[\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})|H_{i}\in[h,h+\epsilon],X_{i}=x,V_{i}=v,W_{i}=w]
. I employ this limit representation to offer an intuitive description of the Theorem
1
estimand as a weighted average over
\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})
, among individuals having
H_{i}
“near”
\tau_{V_{i}}(r)
, in the limit that
\epsilon\downarrow 0
.
62
62

62

One can also establish Theorem
1
intuitively by applying Theorem
2
and letting
x^{\prime}\rightarrow x
(see Footnote
36
).

Sasaki 2015
shows how such outcome-conditioned average derivatives can be written as an explicit integral over the distribution of heterogeneity values
U_{i}\in\mathbbm{R}^{d_{U}}
such that
h(x,U_{i})=\tau_{v}(r)
.

H.3

Proof of Corollary
2

Let
\beta_{r}(x,v,w):=\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})|H_{i}=\tau_{v}(r),x,v,w\right]
. Averaging Eq (
10
) over
X_{i},W_{i}
yields:

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}[

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{j}}P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i},W_{i})]

\displaystyle=-\int dF_{XW}(x,w)\cdot\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot f_{H|XVW}(\tau_{v}(r)|x,v,w)\cdot\beta_{r}(x,v,w)

\displaystyle=-\int dF_{XW}(x,w)\cdot\int dF_{V|XW}(v|x,w)\cdot f_{H|XVW}(\tau_{v}(r)|x,v,w)\cdot\beta_{r}(x,v,w)

\displaystyle=-\int dF_{XVW}(x,v,w)\cdot f_{H|XVW}(\tau_{v}(r)|x,v,w)\cdot\beta_{r}(x,v,w)

\displaystyle=-\int dF_{XVW|H}(x,v,w|\tau_{v}(r))\cdot f_{H}(\tau_{v}(r))\cdot\beta_{r}(x,v,w)

\displaystyle=-\int dF_{V|H}(v|\tau_{v}(r))\cdot f_{H}(\tau_{v}(r))\int dF_{XW|VH}(x,w|v,\tau_{v}(r))\cdot\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(X_{i},U_{i})|H_{i}=\tau_{v}(r),x,v,w\right]

\displaystyle=-\int dF_{V|H}(v|\tau_{v}(r))\cdot f_{H}(\tau_{v}(r))\cdot\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(X_{i},U_{i})|H_{i}=\tau_{V_{i}}(r),V_{i}=v\right]

\displaystyle=-\int dF_{V}(v)\cdot f_{H|V}(\tau_{v}(r)|v)\cdot\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(X_{i},U_{i})|H_{i}=\tau_{V_{i}}(r),V_{i}=v\right]

using EXOG in the second step. Note that by Bayes’ rule:

dF_{V|H-\tau_{V}(r)}(v|0)=f_{H-\tau_{V}(r)|V}(0|v)\cdot\frac{dF_{V}(v)}{f_{H-\tau_{V}(r)}(0)}=f_{H|V}(\tau_{v}(r)|v)\cdot\frac{dF_{V}(v)}{f_{H-\tau_{V}(r)}(0)}

and thus
dF_{V}(v)\cdot f_{H|V}(v|\tau_{v}(r))=f_{H-\tau_{V}(r)}(0)\cdot dF_{V|H-\tau_{V}(r)}(v|0)
, where
f_{H|V}(\tau_{v}(r)|v)=f_{H-\tau_{V}(r)|V}(0|v)
given that
\tau_{V}(r)
is a constant given
V=v
. Note that existence of
f_{H-\tau_{V}(r)}(0)
is guaranteed by Assumption
REG_{j}
, since by integrating item four of
REG_{j}
we know that the density
f_{H|V}
exists and thus the density
f_{H-\tau_{V}(r)|V}
exists as well. Thus:

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}[

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{j}}P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i},W_{i})]

\displaystyle=-\int dF_{V}(v)\cdot f_{H|V}(\tau_{v}(r)|v)\cdot\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(X_{i},U_{i})|H_{i}=\tau_{V_{i}}(r),V_{i}=v\right]

\displaystyle=-f_{H-\tau_{V}(r)}(0)\cdot\int dF_{V|H-\tau_{V}(r)}(v|0)\cdot\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(X_{i},U_{i})|H_{i}-\tau_{V_{i}}(r)=0,V_{i}=v\right]

\displaystyle=-f_{H-\tau_{V}(r)}(0)\cdot\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(X_{i},U_{i})|H_{i}-\tau_{V_{i}}(r)=0\right]=-f_{H-\tau_{V}(r)}(0)\cdot\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(X_{i},U_{i})|H_{i}=\tau_{V_{i}}(r)\right]

H.4

Proof of Theorem
2

I begin with a heuristic overview: the detailed proof is below. The logic of the result is as follows: for a given individual having
V_{i}=v
,
R_{i}
will be less than or equal to
r
when
X_{i}=x^{\prime}
, but not when
X_{i}=x
, if
\Delta_{i}<0
and
h(x,U_{i})\in(\tau_{v}(r),\tau_{v}(r)+|\Delta_{i}|]
. This event increases the value of
P(R_{i}\leq r|x,w^{\prime})-P(R_{i}\leq r|x,w)
. On the other hand,
R_{i}
will be less than or equal to
r
when
X_{i}=x
but not when
X_{i}=x^{\prime}
when
\Delta_{i}>0
and
h(x,U_{i})\in(\tau_{v}(r)-\Delta_{i},\tau_{v}(r)]
. This event instead decreases the value of
P(R_{i}\leq r|x^{\prime},w)-P(R_{i}\leq r|x,w)
. The RHS of Theorem
2
can be written as

\mathbbm{E}\left\{\left.\int_{\tau_{V_{i}}(r)-\Delta_{i}}^{\tau_{V_{i}}(r)}dy\cdot f_{H}(y|\Delta_{i},X_{i}=x,V_{i})\right|W_{i}=w\right\},

which averages over both positive and negative
\Delta_{i}
, covering both cases.

Now let us prove the result of Theorem
2
. By the law of iterated expectations, Lemma
1
, and then EXOG

\displaystyle P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i}=x^{\prime},W_{i}=w)-P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i}=x,W_{i}=w)

\displaystyle=\int dF_{UV|XW}(u,v|x^{\prime},w)\cdot\mathbbm{1}(r(h(x^{\prime},u),v)\leq r)-\int dF_{UV|XW}(u,v|x,w)\cdot\mathbbm{1}(r(h(x,u),v)\leq r)

\displaystyle=\int dF_{UV|XW}(u,v|x^{\prime},w)\cdot\mathbbm{1}(h(x^{\prime},u)\leq\tau_{v}(r))-\int dF_{UV|XW}(u,v|x,w)\cdot\mathbbm{1}(h(x,u)\leq\tau_{v}(r))

\displaystyle=\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\left\{P(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r)|X_{i}=x^{\prime},v,w)-P(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r)|X_{i}=x,v,w)\right\}

\displaystyle=\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\left\{P(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r)|X_{i}=x,v,w)-P(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r)|X_{i}=x,v,w)\right\}

using that
X_{i}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp U_{i}|W_{i},V_{i}
by EXOG in the last step. Thus:

\displaystyle P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i}=x^{\prime},W_{i}=w)-P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i}=x,W_{i}=w)

\displaystyle=\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\left\{P(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r)\textrm{ but not }h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r)|x,v,w)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\hskip 144.54pt-P(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r)\textrm{ but not }h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r)|x,v,w)\right\}

\displaystyle=\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\left\{P(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r)<h(x,U_{i})|x,v,w)-P(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r)<h(x^{\prime},U_{i})|x,v,w)\right\}

\displaystyle=\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\left\{P(h(x,U_{i})\in(\tau_{v}(r),\tau_{v}(r)-\Delta_{i}]|x,v,w)-P(h(x,U_{i})\in(\tau_{v}(r)-\Delta_{i},\tau_{v}(r)]|x,v,w)\right\}

\displaystyle=\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\left\{P(H_{i}\in(\tau_{v}(r),\tau_{v}(r)-\Delta_{i}]|x,v,w)-P(H_{i}\in(\tau_{v}(r)-\Delta_{i},\tau_{v}(r)]|x,v,w)\right\}

\displaystyle=-\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\int dF_{\Delta|XVW}(\Delta|x,v,w)\cdot\left\{P(H_{i}\in(\tau_{v}(r),\tau_{v}(r)-\Delta]|\Delta,x,v,w)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 289.07999pt\left.-P(H_{i}\in(\tau_{v}(r)-\Delta,\tau_{v}(r)]|\Delta,x,v,w)\right\}

\displaystyle=-\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\int dF_{\Delta|XVW}(\Delta|x,v,w)\int_{\tau_{v}(r)-\Delta}^{\tau_{v}(r)}dy\cdot f_{H}(h|\Delta,x,v,w)

\displaystyle=-\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\int dF_{\Delta|VW}(\Delta|x,v,w)\cdot\bar{f}_{H}(\tau_{v}(r)|\Delta,x,v,w)\cdot\Delta

\displaystyle=-\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\mathbbm{E}[\bar{f}_{H}(\tau_{v}(r)|\Delta_{i},x,v,w)\cdot\Delta_{i}|V_{i}=v,W_{i}=w]

\displaystyle=-\mathbbm{E}[\bar{f}_{H}(\tau_{V_{i}}(r)|\Delta_{i},x,V_{i},w)\cdot\Delta_{i}|W_{i}=w]

using EXOG and with the definition
\bar{f}_{H}(y|\Delta,x,v,w):=\frac{1}{\Delta}\int_{y-\Delta}^{y}f_{H}(h|\Delta,x,v,w)dh
.

H.5

Proof of Proposition
1

To fix the scale normalization, suppose that
g(x^{*})=1
for some
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
. Then, note that by the fundamental theorem of calculus, we may write

\displaystyle\log g(x)=\int_{x^{*}}^{x}\nabla\log g(x)\circ dv=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\int_{x^{*}_{j}}^{x_{j}}\partial_{x_{j}}\log g(x_{1},\dots x_{j-1},t,0,\dots,0)dt

where
\circ
denotes a dot product and
dv
traces any continuous path in
\mathcal{X}
from
x^{*}
to
x
, for example the one given after the second equality that integrates over each
x_{j}
in turn.

If all components of
X
are continuous and there are no controls, then note that for any
x\in\mathcal{X}
we can identify
\partial_{x_{j}}g(x)/\partial_{x_{k}}g(x)=\partial_{x_{j}}\mathbbm{E}[R_{i}|x]/\partial_{x_{k}}\mathbbm{E}[R_{i}|x]
for any
j,k\in 1\dots J
by Eq. (
3.4.1
). By assumption that
g(x)
is homogeneous of degree one, we have that
g(\lambda x)=\lambda g(x)
. “Euler’s theorem” of homogeneous functions then implies that
g(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\partial_{x_{j}}g(x)\cdot x_{j}
(this result can be obtained by differentiating
g(\lambda x)=\lambda g(x)
with respect to
\lambda
and evaluating at
\lambda=1
). Thus
\left(\partial_{x_{k}}\log g(x)\right)^{-1}=\frac{g(x)}{\partial_{x_{k}}g(x)}=1+\sum_{j\neq k}\frac{\partial_{x_{j}}g(x)}{\partial_{x_{k}}g(x)}\cdot x_{j}
. We now arrive at a constructive expression for
g(x)
in terms of observables

g(x)=e^{\int_{x^{*}_{j}}^{x_{j}}\left(1+\sum_{j\neq k}\frac{\partial_{x_{j}}\mathbbm{E}[R_{i}|(x_{1},\dots x_{j-1},t,0,\dots,0)]}{\partial_{x_{k}}\mathbbm{E}[R_{i}|(x_{1},\dots x_{j-1},t,0,\dots,0)]}\cdot x_{j}\right)^{-1}dt}

(51)

H.6

Proof of Proposition
3

For any
x
and
x^{\prime}
that differ in component
X_{j}
only:

\displaystyle P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i}=x^{\prime},A_{i})-P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i}=x,A_{i})

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt=\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(H_{i}\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r))|x^{\prime},A_{i}]-\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(H_{i}\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r))|x,A_{i}]

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt=\int dF_{V|XA}(v|x^{\prime},A_{i})\cdot\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(H_{i}\leq\tau_{v}(r))|x^{\prime},v,A_{i}]-\int dF_{V|XA}(v|x,A_{i})\cdot\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(H_{i}\leq\tau_{v}(r))|x,v,A_{i}]

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt=\int dF_{V|XA}(v|x,A_{i})\cdot\{\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r))|x^{\prime},v,A_{i}]-\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r))|x,v,A_{i}]\}

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt=\int dF_{V|XA}(v|x,A_{i})\cdot\{\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r))-\mathbbm{1}(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r))|x,v,A_{i}]\}

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt=\int dF_{V|XA}(v|x,A_{i})\cdot\{\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r)<h(x,U_{i}))|x,v,A_{i}]\}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-\int dF_{V|XA}(v|x,A_{i})\cdot\{\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r)<h(x^{\prime},U_{i}))|x,v,A_{i}]\}

(52)

where in the second equality I have used that
\{X_{ji}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp V_{i}\}|(A_{i},W_{i})
so that
F_{V|XA}(v|x^{\prime},a)=F_{V|XA}(v|x,a)
for all
a
, and in the fourth equality that
\{X_{ji}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp U_{i}\}|(A_{i},W_{i},V_{i})
so that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r))|X_{i}=x^{\prime},V_{i}=v,A_{i}]=\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r))|X_{i}=x,V_{i}=v,A_{i}]

Given (
52
), we have that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\{P(R_{i}\leq r|x^{\prime},A_{i})-P(R_{i}\leq r|x,A_{i})\}|X_{i}=x]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\int dF_{A|X}(a|x)\cdot a\cdot\int dF_{V|XA}(v|x,a)\cdot\{\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r)<h(x,U_{i})|x,v,a]\}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-\int dF_{A|X}(a|x)\cdot a\cdot\int dF_{V|XA}(v|x,a)\cdot\{\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r)<h(x^{\prime},U_{i})|x,v,a]\}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x,U_{i}))|X_{i}=x]-\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x^{\prime},U_{i}))|X_{i}=x]

and similarly

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}[P(R_{i}\leq r|x^{\prime},A_{i})-P(R_{i}\leq r|x,A_{i})|X_{i}=x]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x,U_{i}))|X_{i}=x]-\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x^{\prime},U_{i}))|X_{i}=x]

Note that assuming the numerator and denominator below both exist, we can write

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\partial_{x_{j}}P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i}=x,A_{i})|X_{i}=x]}{\mathbbm{E}[\partial_{x_{j}}P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i}=x,A_{i})|X_{i}=x]}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\frac{\lim_{x^{\prime}\downarrow x}\frac{1}{||x^{\prime}-x||}\cdot\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\{P(R_{i}\leq r|x^{\prime},A_{i})-P(R_{i}\leq r|x,A_{i})\}|X_{i}=x]}{\lim_{x^{\prime}\downarrow x}\frac{1}{||x^{\prime}-x||}\cdot\mathbbm{E}[P(R_{i}\leq r|x^{\prime},A_{i})-P(R_{i}\leq r|x,A_{i})|X_{i}=x]}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\lim_{x^{\prime}\downarrow x}\frac{\cancel{\frac{1}{||x^{\prime}-x||}}\cdot\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\{P(R_{i}\leq r|x^{\prime},A_{i})-P(R_{i}\leq r|x,A_{i})\}|X_{i}=x]}{\cancel{\frac{1}{||x^{\prime}-x||}}\cdot\mathbbm{E}[P(R_{i}\leq r|x^{\prime},A_{i})-P(R_{i}\leq r|x,A_{i})|X_{i}=x]}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\lim_{x^{\prime}\downarrow x}\frac{\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\{P(R_{i}\leq r|x^{\prime},A_{i})-P(R_{i}\leq r|x,A_{i})\}|X_{i}=x]}{\mathbbm{E}[P(R_{i}\leq r|x^{\prime},A_{i})-P(R_{i}\leq r|x,A_{i})|X_{i}=x]}

where
x^{\prime}
is a sequence of vectors that differ from
x
only in the
j^{th}
component, and I’ve assumed dominated convergence so that we can interchange the limits and expectations.

Thus, by the above:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\partial_{x_{j}}P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i}=x,A_{i})|X_{i}=x]}{\mathbbm{E}[\partial_{x_{j}}P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i}=x,A_{i})|X_{i}=x]}

\displaystyle=\lim_{x^{\prime}\downarrow x}\hskip 11.74988pt\frac{\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x,U_{i}))|X_{i}=x]}{\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x,U_{i}))|X_{i}=x]-\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x^{\prime},U_{i}))|X_{i}=x]}

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt-\lim_{x^{\prime}\downarrow x}\hskip 11.74988pt\frac{\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x^{\prime},U_{i}))|X_{i}=x]}{\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x,U_{i}))|X_{i}=x]-\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x^{\prime},U_{i}))|X_{i}=x]}

(53)

Assume that
\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})
has the same sign for all
i
“uniformly” in the sense that
either the first or the second term above is zero. Suppose for example that
\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})\geq 0
with probability one. Then:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\partial_{x_{j}}P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i}=x,A_{i})|X_{i}=x]}{\mathbbm{E}[\partial_{x_{j}}P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i}=x,A_{i})|X_{i}=x]}

\displaystyle=\lim_{x^{\prime}\downarrow x}\hskip 11.74988pt\frac{\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x,U_{i}))|X_{i}=x]}{\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x,U_{i}))|X_{i}=x]}

\displaystyle=\lim_{x^{\prime}\downarrow x}\hskip 11.74988pt\frac{P(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x,U_{i})|X_{i}=x)\cdot\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}|h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x,U_{i}),X_{i}=x]}{P(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x,U_{i})|X_{i}=x)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{x^{\prime}\downarrow x}\hskip 11.74988pt\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}|h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x,U_{i}),X_{i}=x]=\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}|h(x,U_{i})=\tau_{V_{i}}(r),X_{i}=x]

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}|H_{i}=\tau_{V_{i}}(r),X_{i}=x]

provided that the RHS of the last line is well-defined. Similarly, if
\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})\leq 0
with probability one, then the LHS above evaluates to
\lim_{x^{\prime}\downarrow x}\hskip 11.74988pt\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}|h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x^{\prime},U_{i}),X_{i}=x]=\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}|H_{i}=\tau_{V_{i}}(r),X_{i}=x]
and we thus obtain the same expression.

More generally, if the sign of treatment effects vary by unit:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\partial_{x_{j}}P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i}=x,A_{i})|X_{i}=x]}{\mathbbm{E}[\partial_{x_{j}}P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i}=x,A_{i})|X_{i}=x]}

\displaystyle=\lim_{x^{\prime}\downarrow x}\hskip 11.74988pt\frac{P(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x,U_{i})|X_{i}=x)\cdot\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}|h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x,U_{i}),X_{i}=x]}{P(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x,U_{i})|X_{i}=x)-P(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x^{\prime},U_{i})|X_{i}=x)}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-\lim_{x^{\prime}\downarrow x}\hskip 11.74988pt\frac{P(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x^{\prime},U_{i})|X_{i}=x)\cdot\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}|h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x^{\prime},U_{i}),X_{i}=x]}{P(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x,U_{i})|X_{i}=x)-P(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x^{\prime},U_{i})|X_{i}=x)}

(54)

and the estimand
\frac{\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\partial_{x_{j}}P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i}=x,A_{i})|X_{i}=x]}{\mathbbm{E}[\partial_{x_{j}}P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i}=x,A_{i})|X_{i}=x]}
yields a non-convex combination of
\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}|\mathbbm{1}(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x^{\prime},U_{i}),X_{i}=x]
and
\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}|\mathbbm{1}(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x,U_{i}),X_{i}=x]
.

A sufficient condition for
\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})
to have the same sign for all
i
“uniformly” in the above sense is that for all
x^{\prime}
within some neighborhood of
x
,
h(x^{\prime},U_{i})
is either strictly increasing or strictly decreasing in component
j
of
x^{\prime}
, for all
U_{i}
. Specifically, let
x^{\prime}(\delta)
be the vector
x
but with
\delta
added to the
j^{th}
component. Then, for some
\bar{\delta}>0
, we have we have that
P(h(x,U_{i})\geq h(x^{\prime}(\delta),U_{i}))=1
or
P(h(x,U_{i})\geq h(x^{\prime}(\delta),U_{i}))=0
for any
\delta\leq\bar{\delta}
(i.e.
x^{\prime}
and
x
are sufficiently close). Then given that
\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}|H_{i}=\tau_{V_{i}}(r),X_{i}=x]
is well-defined we have either that
\lim_{x^{\prime}\downarrow x}\frac{P(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x,U_{i})|X_{i}=x)}{P(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x^{\prime},U_{i})|X_{i}=x)}=0
if
\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})\geq 0
with probability one, or
that
\lim_{x^{\prime}\downarrow x}\frac{P(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x^{\prime},U_{i})|X_{i}=x)}{P(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x,U_{i})|X_{i}=x)}=0
if
\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})\leq 0
with probability one. In either case one term of (
54
) evaluates to zero and the other to
\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}|H_{i}=\tau_{V_{i}}(r),X_{i}=x]
.

To see that (
36
) holds under the stronger condition that
\{X_{ji}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp(A_{i},U_{i},V_{i})\}|W_{i}
, we have in this case by similar steps as above:

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(R_{i}\leq r)|X_{i}=x^{\prime}]-\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(R_{i}\leq r)|X_{i}=x]

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt=\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(H_{i}\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r))|x^{\prime}]-\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(H_{i}\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r))|x]

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt=\int dF_{VA|X}(v,a|x^{\prime})\cdot a\cdot\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(H_{i}\leq\tau_{v}(r))|x^{\prime}]-\int dF_{VA|X}(v,a|x)\cdot a\cdot\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(H_{i}\leq\tau_{v}(r))|x]

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt=\int dF_{VA|X}(v,a|x)\cdot a\cdot\{\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r))|x^{\prime}]-\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r))|x]\}

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt=\int dF_{VA|X}(v,a|x)\cdot a\cdot\{\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r))-\mathbbm{1}(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r))|x]\}

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt=\int dF_{VA|X}(v,a|x)\cdot a\cdot\{\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r)<h(x,U_{i}))|x]\}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-\int dF_{VA|X}(v,a|x)\cdot a\cdot\{\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r)<h(x^{\prime},U_{i}))|x]\}

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt=\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\{\mathbbm{1}(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x,U_{i}))-\mathbbm{1}(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r)<h(x^{\prime},U_{i}))\}|X_{i}=x]

(55)

using that
\{X_{ji}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp(V_{i},A_{i})\}|W_{i}
so that
F_{VA|X}(v,a|x^{\prime})=F_{VA|X}(v,a|x)
for all
a
in the third equality. Similarly:

\displaystyle P(R_{i}\leq r)|X_{i}=x^{\prime})-P(R_{i}\leq r)|X_{i}=x)

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt=\mathbbm{E}[\mathbbm{1}(h(x^{\prime},U_{i})\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)<h(x,U_{i}))-\mathbbm{1}(h(x,U_{i})\leq\tau_{v}(r)<h(x^{\prime},U_{i}))|X_{i}=x]

(56)

And thus

\frac{\partial_{x_{j}}\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(R_{i}\leq r)|X_{i}=x]}{\partial_{x_{j}}P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i}=x)}=\lim_{x^{\prime}\downarrow x}\frac{\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(R_{i}\leq r)|X_{i}=x^{\prime}]-\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(R_{i}\leq r)|X_{i}=x]}{P(R_{i}\leq r)|X_{i}=x^{\prime})-P(R_{i}\leq r)|X_{i}=x)}

under suitable regularity conditions to take the derivative outside of the expectation. Given (
55
) and (
56
), the above yields the same estimand as (
53
), again simplifying to
\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}|h(x,U_{i})=\tau_{V_{i}}(r),X_{i}=x]
given the common sign of derivative
\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})
across all individuals
i
.

H.7

Proof of Corollary
4

The proof will make use of the following lemma:

Lemma
.

Let
A
and
B
be two independent random variables defined on a common probability space, and
g
a measurable function of
A
. Let
C=A-B
. Then
A\perp\!\!\!\!\perp C
iff
B
is distributed uniformly on its support.

Proof.

Let the supports of
A
,
B
and
C
be
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
, and
\mathcal{C}
. Note that by the law of iterated expectations

P(C\leq c|A=a)=P(g(a)-B\leq c|A=a)=P(B\geq g(a)-c)

using independence between
A
and
B
.

Now consider the case that
B
is uniformly distributed on
\mathcal{B}
.
P(C\leq c|A=a)=1-F_{B}(g(a)-c)
and
C
thus has a density conditional on
A
:

\displaystyle f_{C|A=a}(c)=\partial_{c}P(C\leq c|A=a)=f_{B}(g(a)-c)

(57)

Since
f_{B}(g(a)-c)
is constant on
\mathcal{B}
,
f_{C|A=a}(c)
does not depend on
a
for any
c\in\mathcal{C}
, and hence
C
and
A
are independent.

In the other direction, note that the RHS of (
57
)
will
depend on
a
for some
c
, provided that
f_{B}(b)\neq f_{B}(b^{\prime})
for some
b,b^{\prime}\in\phi(\mathcal{B})
, where we say that
f_{B}(b)\neq f_{B}(b^{\prime})
if
f_{B}(t)
and
f_{B}(t^{\prime})
both exist or have different values, or if the derivative of
F_{B}(\cdot)
does not exist at one of the two points. Suppose that
F_{B}(\cdot)
is differentiable at
b
but not at
b^{\prime}
. Then for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
P(C\leq c|A=a)
is differentiable at
c=g(a)-b
but not at
c=g(a)-b^{\prime}
, and hence
C
and
A
are not independent. Suppose instead that
F_{B}(\cdot)
is differentiable at
b
and
b^{\prime}
but
f_{B}(b)\neq f_{B}(b^{\prime})
. Note that for any
b\in\mathcal{B}
at which
F_{B}
is differentiable, and any
c\in\mathcal{C}
, the conditional density
f_{C|g(A)=c+b}(c)
exists and is equal to
f_{B}(b)
. Thus
f_{C|g(A)=c+b}(c)\neq f_{C|g(A)=c+b^{\prime}}(c)
, and again
C
and
A
are not independent.
∎

Let
\tau_{ri}=\tau_{V_{i}}(r)
and define
\tau_{i}
to be a vector of
\tau_{ri}
across
r\in\mathcal{R}
. Given
(U_{i},V_{i})\perp\!\!\!\!\perp X_{i}|W_{i}
. If this independence assumption holds with
V_{i}=\tau_{i}
, then it also holds with
\tau_{i}(r)
, and we can rewrite Eq. (
10
) as a one-dimensional integral over
\tau_{ri}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{j}}P(R_{i}\leq r|x,w)

\displaystyle=-\int dF_{\tau_{r}|W}(t|w)\cdot f_{h(x,U)}(t|\tau_{ri}=t,x,w)\cdot\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})|h(x,U_{i})=t,\tau_{ri}=t,x,w\right]

\displaystyle=-\int dF_{\tau_{r}|W}(t|w)\cdot f_{h(x,U)}(t|\tau_{ri}=t,x,w)\cdot\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})|h(x,U_{i})=t,h(x,U_{i})-\tau_{ri}=0,x,w\right]

Under the assumption that
\tau_{ri}\sim Unif[\ell_{w},u_{w}]
and
V_{i}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp U_{i}|X_{i},W_{i}
, we can replace
dF_{\tau_{r}|W}(t|w)
with
\frac{dt}{\mu_{w}-\ell_{w}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(\ell_{w}\leq t\leq\mu_{w})
and we have by the Lemma above that
U_{i}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp\{h(x,U_{i})-\tau_{ri}\}|X_{i}=x,W_{i}=w
. Thus:

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{j}}P(R_{i}\leq r|x,w)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\mu_{w}-\ell_{w}}\cdot\int_{\ell_{w}}^{u_{w}}dt\cdot f_{h(x,U)}(t|x,w)\cdot\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})|h(x,U_{i})=t,x,w\right]

since provided that
t\in\textrm{supp}\{\tau_{ri}|W_{i}=w\}=[\ell_{w},\mu_{w}]
,
f_{h(x,U)}(t|\tau_{ri}=t,x,w)=f_{h(x,U)}(t|x,w)
and
\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})|h(x,U_{i})=t,h(x,U_{i})-\tau_{ri}=0,x,w\right]=\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})|h(x,U_{i})=t,x,w\right]
. Meanwhile:

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})|x,w\right]

\displaystyle=\int dt\cdot f_{h(x,U)}(t|x,w)\cdot\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})|h(x,U_{i})=t,x,w\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\ell_{w}}^{u_{w}}dt\cdot f_{h(x,U)}(t|x,w)\cdot\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})|h(x,U_{i})=t,x,w\right]

using that
\textrm{supp}\{h(x,U_{i})\}\subseteq[\mu_{w},\ell_{w}]
in the second equality.

Combining, we have that
\partial_{x_{j}}P(R_{i}\leq r|x,w)=-\frac{1}{\mu_{w}-\ell_{w}}\cdot\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})|x,w\right]
.

H.8

Proof of Proposition
4

Using integration by parts and IDR:

\displaystyle P(R_{i}\leq r|X_{i}=x,W_{i}=w)=\int_{h}P(r(h,V_{i})\leq r|H_{i}=h,X_{i}=x,W_{i}=w)\cdot dF_{H|XW}(h|x,w)

\displaystyle=\int_{h}P(r(h,V_{i})\leq r|W_{i}=w)\cdot dF_{H|XW}(h|x,w)

\displaystyle=\left.F_{H|XW}(h|x,w)P(r(h,V_{i})\leq r)\right|_{h}-\int_{h}F_{H|XW}(h|x,w)\cdot\frac{d}{dh}P(r(h,V_{i})\leq r|W_{i}=w)\cdot dh

This implies that

\displaystyle P(R_{i}\leq r|x^{\prime},w)

\displaystyle-P(R_{i}\leq r|x,w)

\displaystyle=-\int_{h}\left\{F_{H|XW}(h|x^{\prime},w)-F_{H|X}(h|x,w)\right\}\cdot\frac{d}{dh}P(r(h,V_{i})\leq r|W_{i}=w)

\displaystyle=-\int_{h}\left\{F_{H|XW}(h|x^{\prime},w)-F_{H|XW}(h|x,w)\right\}\cdot\frac{d}{dh}P(h\leq\tau_{V_{i}}(r)|W_{i}=w)

\displaystyle=\int_{h}\left\{F_{H|X}(h|x^{\prime})-F_{H|X}(h|x)\right\}\cdot f_{\tau_{V}|W}(h|w)

since the first term does not depend on
x
.

H.9

Proof of Proposition
5

The following sequence of steps uses the law of iterated expectations, then IDR, then
\mathbbm{E}[A_{i}|X_{i}=x,W_{i}=w]=\int_{0}^{1}Q_{A|X=x,W=w}(u)\cdot du
for any random variable
A
, and finally that
Q_{r(H,v)|X=x^{\prime},W=w}(u)=r(Q_{H|X=x^{\prime},W=w}(u),v)
since
r(\cdot,v)
is weakly increasing and left-continuous for all
v

(
Hosseini 2010
)
:

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}[R_{i}|x^{\prime},w]-\mathbbm{E}[R_{i}|x,w]

\displaystyle=\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\mathbbm{E}[r(H_{i},v)|x^{\prime},v,w]-\mathbbm{E}[r(H_{i},v)|x,v,w]

\displaystyle=\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\left\{\mathbbm{E}[r(H_{i},v)|x^{\prime},w]-\mathbbm{E}[r(H_{i},v)|x,w]\right\}

\displaystyle=\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\left\{Q_{r(H,v)|X=x^{\prime},W=w}(u)-Q_{r(H,v)|X=x,W=w}(u)\right\}du

\displaystyle=\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\left\{r(Q_{H|X=x^{\prime},W=w}(u),v)-r(Q_{H|X=x,W=w}(u),v)\right\}du

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\left[\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\left\{r(Q_{H|X=x^{\prime},W=w}(u),v)-r(Q_{H|X=x,W=w}(u),v)\right\}\right]du

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\bar{r}^{\prime}_{x^{\prime},x}(u)\cdot\left\{Q_{H|X=x^{\prime},W=w}(u)-Q_{H|X=x,W=w}(u)\right\}du

where the interchange of integrals is warranted provided that each of
\mathbbm{E}[R_{i}|x^{\prime},w]
and
\mathbbm{E}[R|x,w]
are finite, because

\displaystyle\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)

\displaystyle\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\left|r(Q_{H|X=x^{\prime},W=w}(u),v)-r(Q_{H|X=x,W=w}(u),v)\right|du

\displaystyle\hskip 21.68121pt\leq\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\int_{0}^{1}\left|r(Q_{H|X=x^{\prime},W=w}(u),v)|+|r(Q_{H|X=x,W=w}(u),v)\right|du

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt=\mathbbm{E}[|R_{i}||x^{\prime},w]-\mathbbm{E}[|R_{i}||x,w]<\infty

Note as well that
\bar{r}^{\prime}_{x^{\prime},x}(u)\cdot\left\{Q_{H|X=x^{\prime},W=w}(u)-Q_{H|X=x,W=w}(u)\right\}
is always well-defined and equal to
r(Q_{H|X=x^{\prime},W=w}(u),v)-r(Q_{H|X=x,W=w}(u),v)
, because
r(Q_{H|X=x^{\prime},W=w}(u),v)\neq r(Q_{H|X=x,W=w}(u),v)
implies that
Q_{H|X=x^{\prime},W=w}(u)\neq Q_{H|X=x,W=w}(u)
.

H.10

Proof of Proposition
6

By the law of iterated expectations:
\mathbbm{E}[R_{i}|X_{i}=x,W_{i}=w]=\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\int dh\cdot r(h,v)\cdot f_{H}(h|x,v,w)
. Now use REG to move the derivative inside the integral:

\partial_{x_{j}}\mathbbm{E}[R_{i}|X_{i}=x,W_{i}=w]=\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\int dh\cdot r(h,v)\cdot\partial_{x_{j}}f_{H}(h|x,v,w)

Theorem 1 of
Kasy 2022
(for a one-dimensional outcome) implies that

\partial_{x_{j}}f_{H}(h|x,v,w)=-\frac{\partial}{\partial h}\left\{f_{H}(h|x,v,w)\cdot\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})|H_{i}=h,x,v,w\right]\right\}
. Thus

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{j}}\mathbbm{E}[R_{i}|x,w]=-\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\int dh\cdot r(h,v)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial h}\left\{f_{H}(h|x,v,w)\cdot\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})|H_{i}=h,x,v,w\right]\right\}

Now use integration by parts, applying the assumed boundary condition eliminates the first term, establishing the result:

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{j}}\mathbbm{E}[R_{i}|x,w]

\displaystyle=0+\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\int dh\cdot r^{\prime}(h,v)\cdot f_{h}(h|x,v,w)\cdot\mathbbm{E}\left[\partial_{x_{j}}h(x,U_{i})|H_{i}=h,x,v,w\right]

H.11

Proof of Proposition
7

With the substitution
h=\tau_{v}(r)
,
dr=r^{\prime}(h,v)\cdot dh
:

\displaystyle\sum_{r}\int_{\tau_{v}(r)-\Delta}^{\tau_{v}(r)}dy\cdot f_{H}(y|\Delta,x,v,w)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R}}{{\rightarrow}}\bar{R}\cdot\int dr\int_{\tau_{v}(r)-\Delta}^{\tau_{v}(r)}dy\cdot f_{H}(y|\Delta,x,v,w)

\displaystyle=\bar{R}\cdot\int dh\cdot r^{\prime}(h,v)\int_{h-\Delta}^{h}dy\cdot f_{H}(y|\Delta,x,v,w)=\bar{R}\cdot\int dy\int_{y}^{y+\Delta}dh\cdot r^{\prime}(h,v)\cdot f_{H}(y|\Delta,x,v,w)

\displaystyle=\bar{R}\cdot\int dy\cdot\Delta\cdot\bar{r}^{\prime}(y,\Delta,v)\cdot f_{H}(y|\Delta,x,v,w)=\Delta\cdot\bar{R}\cdot\mathbbm{E}[\bar{r}^{\prime}(H_{i},\Delta,v)|\Delta_{i}=\Delta,X_{i}=x,V_{i}=v,W_{i}=w]

where
\bar{r}^{\prime}(y,\Delta,v):=\frac{1}{\Delta}\int_{y}^{y+\Delta}r^{\prime}(h,v)dh
. Thus:

\displaystyle\mathbbm{E}

\displaystyle[R_{i}|X_{i}=x^{\prime},W_{i}=w]-\mathbbm{E}[R_{i}|X_{i}=x,W_{i}=w]

\displaystyle=\bar{R}\cdot\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\int dF_{\Delta|XVW}(\Delta|x,v,w)\cdot\Delta\cdot\mathbbm{E}[\bar{r}^{\prime}(H_{i},\Delta,v)|\Delta_{i}=\Delta,X_{i}=x,V_{i}=v,W_{i}=w]

\displaystyle=\bar{R}\cdot\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\int dF_{\Delta|XVW}(\Delta|x,v,w)\cdot\mathbbm{E}[\Delta\cdot\bar{r}^{\prime}(H_{i},\Delta,v)|\Delta_{i}=\Delta,X_{i}=x,V_{i}=v,W_{i}=w]

\displaystyle=\bar{R}\cdot\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\mathbbm{E}\left[\left.\mathbbm{E}[\Delta_{i}\cdot\bar{r}^{\prime}(H_{i},\Delta_{i},V_{i})|\Delta_{i}=\Delta,X_{i}=x,V_{i}=v]\right|X_{i}=x,V_{i}=v\right]

\displaystyle=\bar{R}\cdot\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\mathbbm{E}[\Delta_{i}\cdot\bar{r}^{\prime}(H_{i},\Delta_{i},V_{i})|X_{i}=x,V_{i}=v,W_{i}=w]

\displaystyle=\bar{R}\cdot\mathbbm{E}[\Delta_{i}\cdot\bar{r}^{\prime}(H_{i},\Delta_{i},V_{i})|X_{i}=x,W_{i}=w]

Note that if we assume that
\Delta_{i}
and
\bar{r}^{\prime}(H_{i},\Delta_{i},V_{i})
are uncorrelated conditional on
X_{i}=x,W_{i}=w
, this reduces to

\mathbbm{E}[R_{i}|X_{i}=x^{\prime}]-\mathbbm{E}[R_{i}|X_{i}=x]=\bar{R}\cdot\mathbbm{E}[\Delta_{i}|X_{i}=x]\cdot\mathbbm{E}[\bar{r}^{\prime}(H_{i},\Delta_{i},V_{i})|X_{i}=x]

H.12

Proof of Proposition
8

Starting with Proposition
7
, observe that
\bar{r}^{\prime}(y,\Delta,v):=\frac{1}{\Delta}\int_{y}^{y+\Delta}r^{\prime}(h,v)dh
is equal to

r^{\prime}(v)\cdot\begin{cases}\frac{y-(\ell(v)-\Delta)}{|\Delta|}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(y\in[\ell(v)-\Delta,\ell(v)])+\mathbbm{1}(y\in[\ell(v),\mu(v)-\Delta])\\
\hskip 144.54pt+\frac{\mu(v)-y}{\Delta}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(y\in[\mu(v)-\Delta,\mu(v)])&\textrm{ if }\Delta>0\\
\frac{y-\ell(v)}{\Delta}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(y\in[\ell(v),\ell(v)+|\Delta|])+\mathbbm{1}(y\in[\ell(v)+|\Delta|,\mu(v)])\\
\hskip 144.54pt+\frac{\mu(v)+|\Delta|-y}{|\Delta|}\cdot\mathbbm{1}(y\in[\mu(v),\mu(v)+|\Delta|])&\textrm{ if }\Delta<0\end{cases}

where
r^{\prime}(v)=\frac{|\mathcal{R}|}{\ell(v)-\mu(v)}
. To ease notation, let us for the moment make the conditioning implicit and let
f(y)
denote
f_{H}(y|\Delta,x,v,w)
and
F(y)
the corresponding conditional CDF. Let us keep
v
also implicit in both
\ell
and
\mu
. If we let
\theta
denote the quantity
\frac{1}{r^{\prime}(v)}\int dy\cdot\bar{r}^{\prime}(y,\Delta,v)
for a fixed
\Delta
, then:

\displaystyle\theta=\begin{cases}[F(\ell)-F(\ell-\Delta)]\mathbbm{E}\left[\left.\frac{H_{i}-(\ell-\Delta)}{\Delta}\right|H_{i}\in[\ell-\Delta,\ell]\right]+F(\mu-\Delta)\\
\hskip 72.26999pt-F(\ell)+[F(\mu)-F(\mu-\Delta)]\mathbbm{E}\left[\left.\frac{\mu-H_{i}}{\Delta}\right|H_{i}\in[\mu-\Delta,\mu]\right]&\textrm{ if }\Delta>0\\
[F(\ell+|\Delta|)-F(\ell)]\mathbbm{E}\left[\left.\frac{H_{i}-\ell}{|\Delta|}\right|H_{i}\in[\ell,\ell+|\Delta|]\right]+F(\mu)\\
\hskip 72.26999pt-F(\ell+|\Delta|)+[F(\mu+|\Delta|)-F(\mu)]\mathbbm{E}\left[\left.\frac{\mu+\Delta-H_{i}}{|\Delta|}\right|H_{i}\in[\mu,\mu+|\Delta|]\right]&\textrm{ if }\Delta<0\end{cases}

(58)

To get a lower bound on
\theta
, we use the assumption that
f(y)
is increasing on the interval
[\ell-|\Delta|,\ell+|\Delta|]
, as well as decreasing on the interval
[\mu-|\Delta|,\mu+|\Delta|]
:

\displaystyle\theta\geq

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}[F(\ell)-F(\ell-\Delta)]+F(\mu-\Delta)-F(\ell)+\frac{1}{2}[F(\mu)-F(\mu-\Delta)]&\textrm{ if }\Delta>0\\
\frac{1}{2}[F(\ell+|\Delta|)-F(\ell)]+F(\mu)-F(\ell+|\Delta|)+\frac{1}{2}[F(\mu+|\Delta|)-F(\mu)]&\textrm{ if }\Delta<0\end{cases}

\displaystyle\hskip 101.17755pt=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}[F(\mu-\Delta)-F(\ell-\Delta)]+\frac{1}{2}[F(\mu)-F(\ell)]&\textrm{ if }\Delta>0\\
\frac{1}{2}[F(\mu+|\Delta|)-F(\ell+|\Delta|)]+\frac{1}{2}[F(\mu)-F(\ell)]&\textrm{ if }\Delta<0\end{cases}

\displaystyle\hskip 101.17755pt=\frac{1}{2}[F(\mu-\Delta)-F(\ell-\Delta)]+\frac{1}{2}[F(\mu)-F(\ell)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[F(\mu(v)|\Delta,x^{\prime},v)-F(\ell(v)|\Delta,x^{\prime},v)]+\frac{1}{2}[F(\mu(v)|\Delta,x,v,w)-F(\ell(v)|\Delta,x,v,w)],

reintroducing conditioning values with the notation
F(\cdot|\Delta,x,v,w):=F_{H|\Delta XVW}(\cdot|\Delta,x,v,w)
. A lower bound on the weight
\Pi_{x,x^{\prime}}
on causal effects in
\mathbbm{E}[R_{i}|x^{\prime},w]-\mathbbm{E}[R_{i}|x,w]
can thus given by averaging over
V_{i}
(c.f. Proposition
7
):

\displaystyle\Pi_{x,x^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\geq\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\int dF(\Delta|x,v,w)\cdot\left\{\frac{1}{2}[F(\mu(v)|\Delta,x^{\prime},v,w)-F(\ell(v)|\Delta,x^{\prime},v,w)]\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 216.81pt\left.+\frac{1}{2}[F(\mu(v)|\Delta,x,v,w)-F(\ell(v)|\Delta,x,v,w)]\right\}

Note that this exactly the same as the average between the weights
\Pi_{x}
and
\Pi_{x^{\prime}}
corresponding to using continuous variation at
X_{i}=x
and
X_{i}=x^{\prime}
, respectively. For example (c.f. Eq.
44
):

\displaystyle\Pi_{x}

\displaystyle=\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\int dF(\Delta|x,v,w)\cdot[F(\mu(v)|\Delta,x,v,w)-F(\ell(v)|\Delta,x,v,w)]

This leads to the lower bound of
\Pi_{x,x^{\prime}}/(\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{x}+\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{x^{\prime}})\geq 1
in Proposition
7
.

Now, to obtain an upper bound, notice that an upper bound on
\theta
occurs if we imagine putting all of the mass in each of the interval conditional expectations in (
58
) to the right in the intervals that depend on
\ell
, and at the left end for the intervals that depend on
\mu
. Then:

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}\cancel{F(\ell)}-F(\ell-\Delta)+\cancel{F(\mu-\Delta)}-\cancel{F(\ell)}+F(\mu)-\cancel{F(\mu-\Delta)}&\textrm{ if }\Delta>0\\
\cancel{F(\ell+|\Delta|)}-F(\ell)+\cancel{F(\mu)}-\cancel{F(\ell+|\Delta|)}+F(\mu+|\Delta|)-\cancel{F(\mu)}&\textrm{ if }\Delta<0\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}F(\mu)-F(\ell-\Delta)&\textrm{ if }\Delta>0\\
=F(\mu+|\Delta|)-F(\ell)&\textrm{ if }\Delta<0\end{cases}=\begin{cases}F(\mu(v)|\Delta,x,v,w)-F(\ell(v)|\Delta,x^{\prime},v,w)&\textrm{ if }\Delta>0\\
F(\mu(v)|\Delta,x^{\prime},v,w)-F(\ell(v)|\Delta,x,v,w)&\textrm{ if }\Delta<0\end{cases}

where I’ve used that
F(y|\Delta,x^{\prime},v,w)=F(y-\Delta|\Delta,x,v,w)
in the last step. An upper bound for
\theta
that applies to both cases can be obtained by adding them together:

\theta\leq F(\mu(v)|\Delta,x,v,w)-F(\ell(v)|\Delta,x,v,w)+F(\mu(v)|\Delta,x^{\prime},v,w)-F(\ell(v)|\Delta,x^{\prime},v,w)

(59)

where I’ve used that
F(\mu)\geq F(\ell-\Delta)
and
F(\mu+|\Delta|)\geq F(\ell)
are implied by the assumption that
f(y)
is increasing on the interval
[\ell-|\Delta|,\ell+|\Delta|]
, while decreasing on the interval
[\mu-|\Delta|,\mu+|\Delta|]
, which implies that
\mu-|\Delta|\geq\ell+|\Delta|
.

Thus, an upper bound on the weight
\Pi_{x,x^{\prime}}
on causal effects in
\mathbbm{E}[R_{i}|x^{\prime},w]-\mathbbm{E}[R_{i}|x,w]
is:

\displaystyle\Pi_{x,x^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\geq\int dF_{V|W}(v|w)\cdot\int dF_{\Delta|XVW}(\Delta|x,v,w)\cdot\left\{F(\mu(v)|\Delta,x^{\prime},v,w)-F(\ell(v)|\Delta,x^{\prime},v,w)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 216.81pt\left.+F(\mu(v)|\Delta,x,v,w)-F(\ell(v)|\Delta,x,v,w)\right\}

leading to the upper bound of
\Pi_{x,x^{\prime}}/(\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{x}+\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{x^{\prime}})\leq 2
in Proposition
7
.

Now consider the final condition in Proposition
7
. That
\Pi_{x,x^{\prime}}/\Pi_{x}\geq 1/2
follows from the above since
F(\mu(v)|\Delta,x^{\prime},v,w)-F(\ell(v)|\Delta,x^{\prime},v,w)\geq 0
for all
\Delta,x,v,w
. For the upper bound we have

\displaystyle\frac{\Pi_{x}}{\Pi_{x,x^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\mathbbm{E}\left\{\left.\frac{NB(x,V_{i},w)}{\mu(V_{i})-\ell(V_{i})}\right|X_{i}=x,W_{i}=w\right\}}{\mathbbm{E}\left[\left.\frac{1}{\mu(V_{i})-\ell(V_{i})}\right|\right]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbbm{E}\left[\left.\frac{1}{\mu(V_{i})-\ell(V_{i})}\right|X_{i}=x,W_{i}=w\right]\cdot NB(x,w)-Cov\left[\left.\frac{1}{\mu(V_{i})-\ell(V_{i})},NB(x,V_{i},w)\right|X_{i}=x,W_{i}=w\right]}{\mathbbm{E}\left\{\left.\frac{1}{\mu(V_{i})-\ell(V_{i})}\right|X_{i}=x,W_{i}=w\right\}}

\displaystyle\geq NB(x,w)-\sqrt{\frac{Var\left[\left.\frac{1}{\mu(V_{i})-\ell(V_{i})}\right|X_{i}=x,W_{i}=w\right]}{\mathbbm{E}\left\{\left.\frac{1}{\mu(V_{i})-\ell(V_{i})}\right|X_{i}=x,W_{i}=w\right\}^{2}}\cdot Var\left[\left.NB(x,V_{i},w)\right|X_{i}=x,W_{i}=w\right]}

\displaystyle\geq NB(x,w)-Var\left[\left.NB(x,V_{i},w)\right|X_{i}=x,W_{i}=w\right]

\displaystyle\geq NB(x,w)-NB(x,w)\cdot(1-NB(x,w))=NB(x,w)^{2}

where
NB(x,v,w):=P(0<R_{i}<\bar{R}|x,v,w)=P(\ell(V_{i})\leq H_{i}\leq\mu(V_{i})|x,v,w)
and
NB(x,w)=\mathbbm{E}[NB(x,V_{i},w)|x,w]
is the observable probability of not bunching given
(X_{i},W_{i})=(x,w)
. The third inequality uses the assumption that
\frac{Var\left[\left.\frac{1}{\mu(V_{i})-\ell(V_{i})}\right|x,w\right]}{\mathbbm{E}\left\{\left.\frac{1}{\mu(V_{i})-\ell(V_{i})}\right|x,w\right\}^{2}}\leq Var\left[\left.NB(x,V_{i},w)\right|x,w\right]
and the last one that
Var\left[\left.NB(x,V_{i},w)\right|x,w\right]\leq NB(x,w)\cdot(1-NB(x,w))
since
NB(x,v,w)\in[0,1]
for all
x,v,w
.

From this notation, we obtain the form written in Proposition
8
by noting that
NB(X_{i},V_{i},W_{i})=1-\mathcal{B}_{i}
. Note that
Var\left[\left.NB(x,V_{i},w)\right|x,w\right]=Var\left[\left.\mathcal{B}_{i}\right|x,w\right]
.
